lights sightings on 27th June, 1967 and a map showing the location of
the people maling the sightings.

conclusions and any information that you may be able to make available
on this series of sightings would be appreciated by this Department.
These eightinge received guite a lot of publicity and an authoritative
and viable statement as to their likely source could be of great value.
The actual aighting reports have not been included as it 1s
thought that the report sunmarises them adequately.
However, if you
feel it necessary, copies of the sighting zeports can be forwarded to
you•

(Affillating Australian CEO. Societies.)
Under the distinguished Patrenage of Air Marshal Sir George Jones, K.B.E, C.B., D.F932

1967
Dear Sir,
My organisation affiliates the principal
Australian Societies concerned with investigation of
unidentified flying objects (UFO) sighting reports.
Research in UPO's has been hitherto
undertaken principally by defence organisations, such
as the Royal Australian and United States Air Forces.
Of recent months, however, the subject has become one
of general scientific interest following hearings before
a Committee of the U.S. Congress, and published statements
made by Dr. J. Allen Hynek of North Western University
wh was for 18 years U.S. Alr Force Scientifie Consultant
UFO's.
Shortly after the Congressional hearings
the U.S. Air Force announced the making of a grant of
$313,000 (U.S.) to the University of Colorado for the
purpose of an analysis of the UFO problem by a team of
University scientists.
The project has row been organised under
the leadership of Dr. Edward U. Condon of the University
of Colorado's Physics Department.
The arpose of my letter is to enquire
whether the C.S.I.R.O. would consider establishing a
similar investigation in Australia to the Colorado
Project.
No doubt, there are certain advantages to
be gained in such an investigation being made here at
this time, arising from the possibility of exchange of
information, and correlation of procedures between the
two projects.
I have no daubt that full co-operation
can be expected from Colorado University, since I under-
stand that some contact has already been made between

Yours faithfully,

Peter E. Norris

INVESTIGATION OF SIGHTINGS OF UNIDENTIFTED FLYING OBJECTS
As requested in your letter 554/1/30(123) of 3 March 1967, we are
agreeable to our Regional, Area, Local and Interpreter offices co-operating
in the initial investigation of Unidentified Flying Objects. A list of
addresses of these offices is attached.
The co-operation will of course be restricted to comment on
meteorological phenomena and the times of release and trajectories of
meteorological balloons. We cannot undertake to provide astronomical inform-
ation.
Yours faithfully,
dillapille
J. W. LILLYWHITE),
Acting Director of Meteorology.

MEMORANDUM TO :-
The Secretary,
Department
of Air,
Russell Office 1.0.T.
CANBERRA.
INVESTIGATION OF SIGHTINGS OF UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS
Thank you for your memorandum of the 3rd March on
this matter.
1 7la 122
Unfortunately, the organization of C.S.I.R.O. is not
such that it would be possible for the Commanding Officer of
the relevant R.A.A.F. Base investigating an U.F.O. to correspond
directly with people at a regional level.
c.S.I.R.O. 's laboratories cover research work in both
the agricultural and biological sciences and the industrial and
physical sciences, and these laboratories are spread throughout
the Commonwealth.
There is no Officer-in-Charge on a regional
basis as such.
However, it would be possible to nominate officers in
Perth, Adelaide, Sydney, Brisbane, and Canberra through whom the
relevant R.A.A.F. Commanding Officer might get in contact.
Our officer could then get in touch with this office in order
to determine where a specific enquiry might best be directed in
The normal functions of the officers I have in
mind would be administrative, and they would be unable to provide
any technical advice.
However, if you feel the suggestion
would be of assistance, I would
be grateful if you would let
me know.
As mentioned in my memorandum of the 7th November,
, it
would be helpful if correspondence about these matters could
be addressed to me at this office, rather than to the Organization's
office in Canberra.
S. Wilken
RECD.
115 HAR 1967
9
C.A.S.
COMMONWEALTH SCIENTIFIC AND INDUSTRIAL RESEARCH ORGANIZATION, AUSTRALIA

We note that you will be informing your Command formations
of the policy of direct liaison from R.A.A.F. bases to our Regional
authorities in respect of all aspects connected with the investigation
of UFO sightings, as suggested in our memorandum of the 26th October, 1966.
However, there is a further point regarding notification of
UFO sightings about which we would appreciate further clarification.
As you are aware, some reports of UrO sightings are received in this
office, either direct or through our Regional offices, and, in each case,
we refer the report to your headquarters for investigation.
proposed new system we suggest that the following procedures apply i-
when a report is received by one of our Regional offices, it
shall be referred to the Office Connanding the nearest R.A.A.F. Base
(or Comnand in the case of New South Wales and Victoria/Tasmania Region) :-
(i1)
when a report is received directly in this office, it shall be
referred to your headquarters as has been the practice in the past.
4.

that we can include appropriate guidance in the instructions we are
forwarding to our Regional offices.

IN DOTMATION

Seen ln DAFI
Whe agrees
1.
The attached letter is a follow-up to a long telephone call which AI-2 took
recently.
You will note that one of the scientists involved in this UFO investigation
is Dr HYNEK who is stated to be the scientific advisor on UFO's to the United States.
Dr DUGGIN, hovever is acting in an extra- curricular capacity.
2.
You will note that these scientists are mainly interested in the unexplained
UFO's, but as far as I can make out they would like information on all sightings.
As
you know, we already have an official arrangement with OSIRO whereby we can pass
to them any UTO report on which we would like their assistance, so it would really
be only a question of stretching this arrangement slightly to pass them a copy of all
reports.
However, Dr DUGGIN's interest is un-official as far as CSIRO is concerned
and this may cause embarrassment.
3.
These scientists, with all the documents and facilities available to them,
are obviously in a position to assist us in this matter, and though I am not too keen
on releasing the details of the RAAT investigations or anything which may increase
the interest of the general public in this field, I think we should give these
scientists the information they require.
However, although they would like the
information as soon as possible, I recommend that we stick to our present system
for UTO investigations, 1.e. the nearest RAP Base investigates the report and passes
it up through Connand Headquarters to Department of Air.
If we change the system to
allow SIRO to get a copy of the report before it has completed the RAAT process, we
may have two concurrent investigations of the same report.
29 Dec 66
(N.G.
MARSHALL)
Wig Car

CANBERRA, A.C.T.
Dear Mr. McFarlane:
Subject:
Unidentified Flying Objects
The NASA Melbourne Office will be happy to
assist in any way in commenting on sightings of
UFO's as requested in your letter of 28th November.
However, at this time it does not appear likely
that any comments we might make would be
particularly useful.
Satellites launched by NASA operate at such
altitudes and are of such small size that it is
extremely unlikely that any of these would
result in the typical public report of an UFO.
The mere fact that satellites are listed as
"visible" in various periodic listings does not
make them visible in the sense that UFO's are
reported to be.
Spent launch vehicles or spacecraft or parts,
such as nose-cones or aerodynamic fairings,
do of course, re-enter the atmosphere when their
orbital altitudes reduce, but these objects would
behave very like a meteor and again would not be
candidates for the typical public UFO report.
es
RECD.
- 2DEC 1966
CASE
2/...

===== PAGE 49 =====
{"alpha_words": 153, "chars": 998, "method": "ocr", "page": 49, "variant": "plain-vision"}
113
A
- 2-
Only one class of object which NASA is
currently involved with in this country might
lead to public reports of UFo's and these are
the high altitude balloons which we plan to fly
from Mildura. These balloons, with a volume
of 10 million cubic feet, relatively low
altitudes of 140,000 feet maximum, and velocities
corresponding to high upper air winds for periods
of up to 12 hours, might conceivably lead to UFO
reports. NASA is not alone in flying balloons in
this country - in fact we are just now starting -
but all such operations are under the cognizance
of the Department of Supply, including the NASA
operations.
I would therefore like to suggest
that you enlist the services of Mr. M. Ian Homewood,
Assistant Secretary (Projects), Department of
Supply, for his comments on UFO reports, since
his staff is intimately concerned with Australian
high altitude balloon operations.
Sincerely yours,
Widon K. Kenter
Willson H. Hunter
NASA Senior Scientific Representative
in Australia

UNIDENTIFIED PLYING OBJECTS
Sightings of Unidentified Plying Objects (UPO's) have during
recent months been receiving considerable publicity.
Although it is not
considered that any of these sightings give any cause for alarm or reflect
& need to change the official view on this subjeet, it is important that
the system of investigation should make the beat use of resources in
AUSTRALIA.
2.
As you aze no doubt awaze the Royal Australian Air Fozce is
responsible for the investigation of UPO's.
Under existing azrangements
anyone who sights an object which he cannot identify is invited to fill in
a questionnaire which is designed to provide all the details necessary for
an investigation.
The nearest Air Porce base ia then requized to give an
assessment as to the origin of the sighting.
3.
The decision to investigate reports of UPO's at RAAP base level
was made on the grounds that base persomel would be familiar with local
phenomena, weather conditions and aircraft movements which between them
give rise to many UPO reports.
There are occasions however when the
investigation points to some phenomena such as a satellite as the likely
In many instances RAAF units have been able to obtain
information from locel bodies to assist them in their enquiries, but it
is now considered that all authorities that could throw any light on these
incidents should be offloially associated with the investigation system.
Your agreement is therefoze sought to the proposal that whenever
the UPO investigation suggests that your organization might be able to provide
some assistance a copy of the UPO report should be sent to you for your coments.
It is felt that in this way better use could be made of the information available
within your organization and that a moze accurate assessment of the likely cause
of the UPO report could be achieved.
(SGD.) A. B. McFARLANE
(A. B. Matarlane) park.

Dear Mr. MaFarlane,
We shall be glad to cooperate in the identification of what
you refer to as "Unidentified Flying Objects".
If I can be permitted a personal observation I would like
to say the following:
Astronomers, by definition, are (1) trained scientific
observers who spend long hours on mountain tops under completely dark
skies, and (2) familiar with what objects should be present in the dark
sky. Yet, in twenty years of spending a large fraction of the nights
in this way - on mountain tops in Australia, California, and South Africa,
I have not seen any object that could be called "unidentified and flying".
Nor have any of my colleagues on these same mountain tops. Nevertheless,
during the same period, members of the general public, living in the haze
and glare of large cities, and unacquainted with the night sky, are con-
stantly seeing such objects.
Yours sincerely,
012495
d. Eggen
Direator

I have received your request 2/25/1 (29) of
12 October, 1966, regarding investigation of V.F.O. sightings.
I doubt whether this station could be of
much help in this regard, since, because we track deep space
probes, we have very little information on hand regarding
Earth orbiting satellites. These latter spacecraft are tracked
by stations located at Orroral Valley (A.C.T.), Carnarvon (w.A)
and Cooky Creek (QLD). A further station at Honeysuckle Creek
(A.C.T.) will also become operational in the near future.
Although we would be pleased to supply information
within our particular field, I suggest that you contact the
senior NASA Representative (399 Swanston St., Melbourne.)

Attention: Director Air Safety
Investigation Branch
REPORTING AND INVESTIGATING OF AIR SAFETY
INCIDENTS WHERE RAAP AND CIVIL AIRCRAFT ARE INVOLVED
During a liaison visit to this Department between the Director of the
Air Safety Investigation Branch, Department of Civil Aviation and the
Director of Flying Safety, RAAF, the present system of reporting and
investigating air safety incidents, where RAAF and civil aircraft are involved,
was discussed.
As a result of this discussion the enclosed proposal to amend the system
of investigation and reporting between Civil and RAAF authorities was drafted.
Your comments on the acceptability of this proposal are requested.
2 Y

this Departual
During a liaison visit to Depair between the Director of the Air Safety
Investigation Branch, Department of Civil Aviation and the Directorate of Flying
Safety, RAAF, the question of the present system of reporting and investigating
air safety incidents, where RAAF and civil aircraft are involved was discussed
at some length.
2.
It was obvious from this discussion that DA are experiencing difficulty
in implementing the present system of reporting and investigation direct to
Commands. One of the main difficulties is that DCA have no knowledge of which
command an RAAF aircraft may come under. As a result of this discussion the
following proposal is put forward for consideration.
Where air safety incidents occur which involve RAAF and Civil aircraft
the following system of reporting and investigation should apply:-
(a)
RAAF Designated Authorities should be nominated,"
Jen appointments, advised to DCA and written into the
scheme.*

all air safety incidents which occur in their area of
responsibility.

Authorities:-
AOC Operational Command
AOC Support Command

Area.
- for the investigation
and reporting of all
ASIMs in the
Victorian Area,

Thank you for your 400/1/39 of the 30th June, 1966.
Following a close examination of the draft proposals
attached to your memorandum we believe that the principles and
procedures expressed therein will result in an improved system
for reporting and investigating air safety incidents of mutual
interest and therefore we generally agree with them.
there are some areas where we believe the system could be further
improved and we submit the following supplementary proposals for
your consideration i-
In peragraph 1, no R.A.A.F. Authority hes bean allocated
responaibility
for investigating air safety incidents
mutual interest occurring in either Tasmania or Papua/New
We would appreciate your including these states
areas allocated to R.A.A.I. Commends or Bases.
(11)
While agreeing to your proposed sub-division of South
Australia/Northern Territory and Queensland into areas
associated with R.A.A.F. bases, we suggest that the
responaibility for investigating air safety incidents
throughout New South Wales be vested in the Air Officer
Commanding, Operational Command.
We feel that the over-
lapping nature of R.A.A.P. operations in this area
justifies the
designation of a single authority with
ovezall responsibility for investigations throughout
the State.
(111)
In paragraph 3(c) you state that where an incident cannot
be investigated readily by the Authority located in the
the responsibility for the investigation,
area of occurrence. c.A., should be transferred to the Authority
both R.A.A.P. and
in the area where the pilot in based. We believe that the
principal that the responsibility for the investigation remeins
with the authorities situated in the area of occumence,
irrespective of the location of the home base of any airorart
concerned in the incident, should be preserved. Our reasons

===== PAGE 72 =====
{"alpha_words": 271, "chars": 1838, "method": "ocr", "page": 72, "variant": "plain-vision"}
2.
for this are that the majority of the informstion required
for the investigation (e.g. personnel, logs, tapes,
etc.,)
is available only in the area of occurrence,
and the locally
based investigators have a greater knowledge of the procedures
and systems used in their area than investigators located
in other areas.
You propose that notification to D.C.A. of R.A.A.F air
safety incidents be made by A.S.I.M.
that the initial
advice of incidents be passed between
telephone and confirmed subsequently by
written advice. We believe that telephone advice permits
a freer exchange of information concerning the circumstances
of the incidents and promotes discussion of mutual interest
concerning the proposed handling of
investigation.
We alno believe that a ground unit should follow the same
procedure when reporting an air safety incident of a serious
We suggest also that the proposed procedures should apply
when an air safety incident report in submitted by ground
units as well as when submitted by pilots-in-command.
In
this respect we are considering such occurrences as
uncuthorised penetrations of R.A.A.P. air space by civil
aireraft etc.
To clarify the situation we have prepared
a summary embodying
the principles set out in your proposals and
the amendments suggested
in the previous paragraph.
A copy of this summary is attached hereto
for your consideration.
If this summary, as a basic statement of a system is acceptable
to you, we suggest that
we should then separately develop draft
working procedures for our respective instruction documents with
a view to meeting again before they are promulgated to clear away
any ambiguities or inconsistencies before the new system is
implemented.
We look Forward
to receiving your comments on the suggested
course of action.
(E.B. YEOND)
Tor Director-General of Civil Aviation.

Irrespective

prepare a report and submit it, together with any recommendations,
through the normal channels to the appropriate Departmental authority.

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS
Sightings of Unidentified Flying Objects have during recent
months been receiving considerable publicity.
Although it is not considered
that any of these sightings give cause for alarm or reflect a need to change
the official view on this subject, it is important that the system of
investigation should make the best use of resources available in AUSTRALIA.
As you are no doubt aware the Royal Australian Air Force is
responsible for the investigation of reports of Unidentified Plying Objects
(UPOs).
Under existing arrangements anyone who sights an object which he
cannot identify is invited to f1l1 in a questionnaire which is designed to
provide all the details necessazy for an investigation.
Force base is then required to inveatigate the incident and give an assessment
as to the origin of the sighting.
The deciaion to investigate reporta of UPOs at RAAF base level
was made on the grounds that base personnel would be familiar with local
phenomena,

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS
Sightings of Unidentified Flying Objects have during recent
months been receiving considerable publicity.
Although it is not considered
that any of these sightinga give cause for alarm or reflect a need to change
the official view on this subject, it is important that the system of
investigation should make the best use of resources available in AUSTRALIA.
As you are no doubt aware the Royal Australian Air Force is
responsible for the investigation of reporta of Unidentified Flying Objects
Under existing arrangements anyone who sights an object which he
cannot identify is invited to t111 in a questionnaire which is designed to
provide all the details necessary for an investigation.
The nearest Air
Force base 1a then required to investigate the incident and give an assessment
as to the origin of the sighting.
'he decision to investigate reports of UF0s at RAAT base level
as made on the grounds that base personnel would be familiar with loca
phenomena, weather conditions and aircraft movements which between them
give rise to many UFO reports.
There are occasions however when the
investigation points to some phenomena such as a celestial body as the likely
explanation.
In many instances RAAF units have been able to obtain information
and advice from local bodies to assist them in their inquiries, but it is now
considered that all authorities that could throw any light on these incidents
should be officially associated with the investigation system.
Your agreement is therefore sought to the proposal that whenever
the UFO investigation suggests that your organization might be able to provide
some assistance a copy of the UFO report should be sent to you for your
comments.
It is felt that in this way better use could be made of the
information available within your orgenization, and that a more socurate
assessment of the likely cause of the UPO report could be achieved.
(A. B. Molarlane)

Dear Sir,
Thank you for your letter of 23rd March, 1965, in which you
ask for infornation on Australia's official policy towards flying saucers
and unidentified flying objects (UPOs).
2.
The Minister for Air, on behalf of the Australian Government,
is reaponsible for official policy towarde UPOs.
The investigation of
all reports of such objecta is carried out by the Royal Australian Air
Force Directorate of Air Force Intelligence at the Department of Air in
Canberra.
A considerable amount of effort is spent in investigating
each report, and the observer is intervieved by selected RAAR personnel.
It in the experience of Great Britain as well as Australia that nine out
of ten sightinga can be identified and attributed to such sources as
astronomical phenomena, eg, falling meteors or planets, aircraft, weather
balloons or gatellites.
In the United States, where moze time and money
is spent on these investigations, 99 per cent of sightings can be satiafaet-
orily identified. If more effort and money were put into the
investigations, we have no doubt that all such sightings could be
identified and satisfactorily explained.
With regard to the metallio ball found in Australia, it was
eventually show to be a portion of an old fashioned type of refrigerator.
The RAAF has to date neither received nor discovered in
Australia or overseas any evidence to support the belief that the earth
is being observed, visited or threatened by machines from other planeta;
nor is there any evidence to prove the existence of flying saucers.
Yours faithfully,
/(t. B. NoFarlane)
SBORETARY

REPORT OF UFO SIGHTING AT CRESSY TASMANIA
SAA(I)
Further to our 554/1/30 dated 23rd December, 1960, I am enclosing
copies of the Reverend Ionel Browning's report, Wing Commander Gal, Waller'a
investigation, and newspaper clippings referring to this and other incidents.
We are of the opinion that the phenomens was the result of the moon
rise associated with meteorological conditions at the time of the sighting.
On 4th October, 1960 moon rise (full quarter) at Cressy would have been
visible shortly after 1800 hours and in an ESE direction. The objeets
apparently seen were near the sky-line in an easterly direction. The presence
of "saud" type clouds, moving in varying directions due to turbalence in and
around the rain squell near which the objeats were sighted, and the position
of the moon or its reflections, (could havel produced the impression of flying
objects•
As can be seen from Wing Conmander Waller's Investigation, this
report emanated from a genuine and reliable source and this Directorate has no

For your information, the objeots sighted by Mrs. D.A. Webster
and her daughter at about 1630 hours on 18th October 1960 have been
positively identified as a TAA Viscount aireraft enroute to Hobart.
airoraft, which has an iridescent red tail fin, was in the approximate

example of the hysteria which normally follors a much publicised incident

illustrstes the unreliability of untrained observers.

Thank you for your letter of 7th November

scientifio studies to the investigation of
unidentifled flying objects.
Although the RAAT 1s responsible for the
investigation of UPO zeports, as a private citizen
you are at liberty to undertake any research into
this subjeet that you wish. However, 11, as part
of your experimenta you propose to make electronio
transnations of any kind it will be necessary to

recognition or assistance.

UPO research, I can only suggest that this would
depend entirely on your own interest in the subject.

ing but an unrewarding task. Considerable research
has already been carried out by both official and
unofficial authorities on a world-adde beads but
so far, without producing any positive resulta.
Iess than 10% of all reports received cannot be

as to whether or not such research is in fact permissible and/or
desirable.
"Research" would not include any practical experiments involving
projectien of infra-red or other rays; or the transmission of any
radio control impulses or traffic; being merely the collation of
data, analysis of same, and the construction of logical hypotheses
based on findings. (In short, purely paper work) -
May I, then, expect a lead from you, in due course, please?
With Thanks,
Yours Faithiully
(A.G.PARK)
senior Researon Fellow & Principal,
(Late lo.3825, W/O PARK, A.G., O.I.C.
Instrument Laboratory & Technical Library,
R.D.I.U.;N.C.O. 1/c 8igs, 3 Sqân, M.E.)

FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLY
SUMMARY
Reports of unidentified aerial objects (popularly termed "flying
saucers" or "flying dises") have been received by the U,S. Air Force
since mid-1947 from many and diverse sources. Although there was no
evidence that the unexplained reports of unidentified objects constituted
a threat to the security of the U.S., the Air Force deterined that all
reports of unidentified aerial objects should be investigated and evaluated
to determine if "flying saucers" represented technological developments
not mown to this country.
In order to discover any pertinent trend or patter inherent in the
data, and to evaluate or explain any trend or patter found, appropriate
methods of reducing these data from reports of unidentified aerial objects
to a form amenable to scientific appreisal were employed.
In general, the
original data upon which this siudy was based consisted of impressions and
interpretations of apparently unexplainable events, and seldom contained
reliable measurements of physical attributes.
this subjectivity of the
data presented a major limitation to the drawing of significant conclusions,
but did not invalidate the application of scientific methods of study.
The reports received by the U.S. Air Force on unidentified aerial
objects were reduced to IBM punch-card abstracts of the data by means
of logically developed forms and standardized evaluation procedures.
Evaluation of sighting reports, a crucial step in the preparation of
the data for statistical treatment, consisted of an appraisal of the
reports and the subsequent categorization of the object or objects
described in each report. A detailed description of this phase of the
study stresses the careful attempt to maintain complete objectivity
and consistency®
Analysis of the refined and evaluated data derived from the original
reports of sightings consisted of (1) a systematic attempt to febret out
any distinguishing characteristies inherent in the data of any of their
segments,
(2) a concentrated study of any trend or pattern found, and
(3) an attempt to determine the probability that any of the UNKNOWNS
represent observations of technological developments not lown to this
country.
The firet step in the analysis of the data revealed the existence
FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLI

===== PAGE 163 =====
{"alpha_words": 304, "chars": 2120, "method": "ocr", "page": 163, "variant": "plain-vision"}
FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLY
of certain apparent similarities between cases of objeats definitely
identified and those not identified.
Statistical methods of testing
when applied indicated a low probability that these apparent similarities
were significant. An attempt to determine the probability that any of
the UNKNOWNS represented observations of technological developments not
known to this country necessitated a thorough re-examination and 28-
evaluation of the cases of objects not originally identified; this led
to the conclusion that this probability was very small.
The special study which resulted in thia report (Analyeis of Reports
of Unidentified Aerial Objects, 5 May 1955) started in 1953. To provide
the study group with a complete set of tiles, the information cut-oft
date was established as of the end of 1952.
It will accordingly be noted
that the statistics contained in all charts and tables in this report are
terminated with the year 1952. In these charts, 3201 cases have been used.
As the study progressed, a constant program was maintained for the
purpose of making comparisons between the current cases received after
1 January 1953, and those being used for the report.
This was done in
order that any change or significant trend which might arise from current
developments could be incorporated in the suary of this report.
The 1953 and 1954 cases show a general and expected trend of
increasing percentaged in the finally identified categories. They also
show decreasing percentages in categories where there was insufficient
Infornation and those where the phenomena could not be explained.
This
trend had been anticipated in the light of improved reporting and in-
vestigating procedures.
orticial reports on hand at the end of 1954 totaled 4034. Or these,

FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLY
As the study of the current cases progressed, it became increasingly
obvious that if reporting and investigating procedures could be further
Improved, the percentages of those cases which contained insufficient
information and those remaining unexplained would be greatly reduced.
The key to a higher percentage of solutions appeared to be in rapid "on
the spot" investigations by trained personnel. On the basis of this, a
revised program was established by AF Reg. 200-2 Subject:
"Und denti fled
Flying Objects Reporting" (Short I1tlesUPOB) dated 12 August 1954.
January 1955, provided primarily that the 4602d Air Intelligence Service
Squadron (Air Defense Conmand) would carry out all field investigations.
This squadron has sufficient units and is so deployed as to be able to
arrive "on the spot" within a very short time after a report is received.
After treatment by the 4602d AISS, all Information is suppled to the Air
Technical Intelligence Center for final evaluation.
This cooperative
program has resulted, since 1 January 1955, in reducing the insufficient
Information cases to 7% and the unknown cases to 3%, of the totals.
The period 1 January 1955 to 5 May 1955 accounted for 131 unidentified
aerial object reports received. Evaluation percentages of these are as
follows:
Balloons
- 26%
Aircraft
- 21%
Astronomical
- 23%
Other
- 20%
Insufficient Info - 7%
Unknow
- 3%
A11 available data were included in this study which was prepared
by a panel of scientists both in and out of the Air Force,
On the basis
of this study it is believed that all the unidentified aerial objects
could have been explained if more complete observational data had been
available. Insofar as the reported aerial objects which still remain
unexplained are concerned, there exists little information other than
the impressions and interpretations of their observers.
As these
impressions and interpretations have been replaced by the use of
- 3-
FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLY

===== PAGE 165 =====
{"alpha_words": 374, "chars": 2457, "method": "ocr", "page": 165, "variant": "plain-vision"}
FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLI
improved methods of investigation and reporting, and by scientific
analysis, the number of unexnlained cases has decreased rapidly
towards the vanishing point.
Therefore, on the basis of this evaluation of the information, it 1s
considered to be highly improbable that reports or unidentified aerial
objects examined in this study represent observations of technological
developments outside of the range of present-day scientific knowledge.
It is emphasized that there has been a complete lack of any valid evidence
of physical matter in any case of a reported unidentified aerial object.
***
CONCLUSIONS
It can never be absolutely proven that "flying saucere" do not
exciat. This would be true if the data obtained were to include complete
scientific measurements of the attributes of each sighting, as well as .
complete and detailed descriptions of the objects sighted. It might be
possible to demonstrate the exiatence of "flying saucers" with data of
this type, IF they were to excist.
Although the reports considered in this study usually did not con-
tain sedentific measurements of the attributes of each sighting, it was
possible to establish certain valtd conclusions by the application of
statistical methods in the treatment of the data.
Scientifically
evaluated and arranged, the data as a whole did not show any marked
patterns or trends.
The inaccuractes inherent in this type of data,
in addition to the incompleteness of a large proportion of the reports,
may have obscured any patterns or trends that otherwise would have been
evident. This absence of indicative relationships necessitated an
exhaustive study of selected facets of the data in order to draw any
valid conclusions.
A critical examination of the distributions of the important
characteristics of sightings, plus an intensive study of the sightings
evaluated as UNKNO:N, led to the conclusion that a combination of
factors, prineipally the reported maneuvers of the objects and the
unavailability of supplemental data such as airoraft flight plans or
balloon-launching records, resulted in the failure to identify as
KNOWNS most of the reports of objects classified as UNKNOWNS.
An intensive study, aimed at finding a verified example of a
"flying saucer" on at deriving a veritied model or models of "flyin
saucers" (as defined on Page 1)
• Led to the conclusion that neither
goal could be attained using the present data.
- 4 -
FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLY

===== PAGE 166 =====
{"alpha_words": 352, "chars": 2353, "method": "ocr", "page": 166, "variant": "plain-vision"}
FOR OFFICIAL USE ONIX
It is emphasized that there was a complete lack of any valid
evidence consisting of physical matter in any case of a roported
unidentified serial object.
Thus, the probability that any of the UNKNOWNS considered in
this study are "flying saucers" is concluded to be extremely small,
since the most complete and reliable reports from the present data,
when isolated and studied, conclusively failed to reveal even a rough
model, and since the data as a whole failed to reveal any marked
patters or trends.
Therefore, on the basis of this evaluation of the information, it
is considered to be highly improbable that any of the reports of un-
identified aerial objects examined in this study represent observations
of technological developments outside the range of present-day seientific
knowLedge.
1 In June, 1947, Kenneth Arnold, a Boise, Idaho, businessman and
private pilot, publicly reported the now-famous sighting of a
chainlike formation of disc-shaped objects near Mount Rainier,
Washington,
Resulting newspaper publieity of this ineident caught
the publie interest, and, shortly thereafter, a rash of reports of
unidentified aeriel objects spawned the term "flying saucers".
During the years since 1941, many reports of unidentified aerial
objects have been received by the Air Force from many and diverse
sources.
The unfortunate term "flying saucer", or "flying disc", because of
its widespread and indiscriminate use, requires derinition. Many
definitions have been offered, one of the best being that originated
by Dr. J. Allen lynek, Director of the Emerson MeMillin Observatory
of The Ohio State University, who has taken a seient1fic interest in
the problem of unidentified aerial objects since 1949, Dr. Hynek's
definition of the term is "any aerial phenomenon or sighting that
remains unexpleined to the viewer at least long enough for him to
write a report about it" (Hynek, J, A., "Unusual Aerial Phenomena",
Journal of the Optical Society of America, 43(4), PP 311-314,
April 1953). Dr. Hynek, elaborating on his definition, says,
"Each flying saucer, so defined, has associated with it a probable
lifetime, It wonders in the field of public inspection 11ke an
electron in a field of ions, until 'captured' by an explanation
which puts an end to its existence as a 'flying saucer!".

FISD FLYING OBJECTS
Minister.
The Directorate of Air Fozoe Intelligence wrote to Air Ministry
London, and to the Australian Joint Services Staff in Washington on 26th
November 1959 for the latest views of the British and United States Governments
on Unidentified Flying Objects. You may be interested in the replies received
recently.
Air Ministry stated that specifie questions have been asked in the
House of Commons concering individual sightings reported in the press. The
Air Ministry has zeplied to questions as they have occurred and Parliament has
not at any time debated the subject. All aighting reports ocourring in the
United Kingdom have been processed through Air Ministry Intelligence. On an
average 90% of all sightings have been explained by a variety of causes ranging
from meteorologioal balloons and meteors to afrozaft 11ghte. Air Miniatry 1e
confident that the other 10% only remain unexplained because of laok of reliable
infornation.
3.
The Defence Department in Washington stated that "over twelve years
of investigating and evaluating unidentified flying objeot sightings has provided
no evidence to confirm the existence of the popularly-terned "flying saucers",
as interplanetary or interstellar apace ships".
During the period June 1947
and October 1958, 6,000 zeports were investigated. More than 84% of the reported
UPO sightings were definitely established as natural phenomena, hoaxes, birds,
or man-made objeets. Insufficient data was available to thoroughly analyse and
evaluate 14% of the reports and less than 26 were clasaified as unknown.
The
investigations carried out during the last half of 1958 set a record low for
the number of cases classified as "unknown" - 67%.

Madording to latest information tabulated by Air Porce authori-
ties charged with investigation of Unidentified Plying Objects,
1270 new UPO reporta were Investigated during the period July 1,
1957 through July 31, 1958. More than 84 percent of the reported
UPO sightings were definitely established as natural phenomena,
hoaxes, birde, or men-made objects.
Insultielent data was available
to thoroughly analyze and evaluate 14 percent of the reporte and
less than 2 percent were classied as unions.
Although investigations were completed on these "uninown" cades
without positive or final conclusions, nothing was found to sub-
stantlate any olaims that such eightirigs were interplanetary
visitors, or so-called "Flying Saucers
A sighting 1s considered unimown when the report contains suf-
Miolent information to draw at least one valld conclusion from the
facts avatlable but when the description of the reported object or
Its movementa cannot be related to the norm. Refinements in Air
Porce Investigative processes, however, have resulted in a steady
ecline in the number of URO reports placed in this category.
ing the early months of the Air Porcels Investigation, some 20 per-
cent of the reports were classified as unknown.
These unimowns were
reduced to 9 percent in 1953 and 1954, 3 percent in 1955, and 1.8
percent during the first six months of this year. It 1s belleved
that specifio conclusions could be drawn for all
reports if addi-
tional observational date were provided by individuals originating
theue reports.
Also, the Air Porce believes that there wil1 alweys
be a small number or
unknowns due to high altitude phenomena which
are strange to the untrained eye.
The 1270 new reports during the past thirteen months increases
the total Air Force investigations conduoted since June 1947 to

IMMEDIATE RELEASE

10. 798-59
OXford 7-5131
AIR FORCE REPORTS UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECT
SIGHTINGS DECREASE BY FIFTY PERCENT
Over twelve years of investigating and evaluating unidentified
flying object sightings has provided no evidence to confirm the
existence of the popularly termed "flying saucers"
• as interplanet-
ary or interstellar space ships. The total number of sightings
for the first six months of 1959 was only 143. This is in sharp
contrast to the previous six months total of 296 or a little more
than a 50 percent drop in reported sightings.
As in previous compilations, the astronomical category accounted
for the highest percentage of sightings at 45 percent of the total.
The unidentified category amounted to only three cases or 2 percent
of the total sightings for the period.

The Air Force, assigned the responsibility for the Air Defense
of the United States, will continue to investigate all reports of
unusual aerial sightings over the United States, including objects
that may become labeled Unidentified Flying Objects. The services
of qualified scientists and technicians will continue to be
utilized to investigate and analyze reports, and periodie public
statements and rèleases will be made as warranted.

Introduction to the Botanai Reports.
The reports decribed in the following pages constitute
the most remarkable testimony of intensive UPO activity
reported to civilian invest gators in the entire history of
UFO rescarch.
The sightings are unique in that, for the
first time,
credible
witnesses have reported the presence of
humanoid beings associated with UrO's,
One important aspect of the Boianai incidents
is that the
UFO's were observed closely by the witnesses for a total
period of some houra;
accordingly, the reports should be
examined exhaustively for
some clue as to
origin of
the craft, and for possible points of correlation
with other credible reports.
BIOGRAPHICAL DETAILS OF FATHER GILI, THE PRINCIPAL WITNESS:
Aged 31 years.
Married with two children.
Educated
Trinity Granmar School, Melbourne, and St.Francis College,
Brisbane.
Ordained priest of the Anglican Church.
licentiate in Theology.
A qualified teacher,
he completed
over eight years of service as a missionary in New Guinea.
FATHER GILD'S RELIABILITY AS WITNESS:
VESRS investigators found Father Gill at all times co-
operative and obviously deeply interested in UFOle and the
implications of their
Prior to his sightinea he
discounted the possibility of
their existence as manned
craft.
Ho exhibits an impressive scholarly detachment and
nis objectivity is evident in reading the report which.
follows within.
Father Gill still remains reluctant to
speculate as to
the origin or precise nature of the UFO's -
at lect, until he has had an opportunity to complete some
into the subject.
THE CORROBORATIVE WITNESSES:
These numbered up to a total of 38 persons during the
principal sightings at Boianai, 27 of whom signed
original
statement examined by VTSRS investigatore.
The witnesses
all Papuan natives,
several of whom were educated
teachers and medical assistants.
BACKGROUND TO THE PRINCIPAL SIGHTINGS:
The Boianai sightings olimaxed a relatively short but
remarkabiy acute period of UPO activity in the vicinity of
eastern New Guinea.
UFOts were observed by both Papuan
natives and Europeans.
Sightings were reported by educated
Papuans and by totally illiterate natives relatively
untouched by
• western civilisation and quite imorant of
/ "flying saucers"

INTRODUCTION.
These reports of observations together with relevant
material
combine
to describe with fair accuracy what is thought
to have been a series of activites in the sky at nights over
and near Boianai by U.F.O. 's.
Main recordings were made on
the evenings of June 2lst, 26th and 27th.
As chief observer and sole recorder and reporter of the
Boianai phenomena on those dates (at Boianai) I would now
hesitate to re-state without further long consideration
backed by scientific investigation any opinions or theories
as expressed in my personal letters hastily written in that
period and which are included in this present material.
The
value (personal) lies in their subjectiveness against
what I
hope is objective observational data, and
in the complete
change of mind from scepticism to the conviction that the
experience.
sorred by The arnot be papt now ate an erme of
Consideration to the following possible factors,
however,
cannot be overlooked if this paper is to have any value at
all as an attempted objective record.
1.
Hallucination
Sketches in The body of the report may be figment of
imagination or hallucination.
Hallucination may have been
experienced simultaneously by the 27 eye-witnesses whose
names appear on the signature list.
Suggestion as an influence to "witnesses"
There may have been undue influence by suggestion of a
European observer over a less sophesticated or inexperienced
native body:
there may have been influence
nervience of the native body of witnessed the
European observer.
3.
Illusion
It is possible that "freak" atmospheric conditions or
subjectiveness on the part of the observer (s) resulted in a
mis-interpretation of natural phenomena.
4. Unreliable Observers
Inexperience in astronomical observation.
Ignorance
regarding such natural phenomena as falling stars, meteors,
comets,
electrical disturbances in the atmosphere; the
appearance and usual movements of these.
Variation in descriptive evidence from different sources
(a) Mis-interpretation due to inexperience or faulty
observation.
(b) Differences
in interpretations by individual observers
resulting from different experiences.
(c) Variation in distances between reliable observers
and the phenomena sighted.
Independent observers have
noted and
reported sightings from as far as twenty
miles from Boianad, the source of activity.

report, theory and hypothesis should be made with note to

what they thought they saw.

Dear David,
Have a look at this extraordinary data.
I am almost
convinced about the "visitation" theory.
a number of reports over the months, from reliable witnesses.
peculiar thing about these most recent reports is that the UFO's
seem to be stationary at Boianai or to travel from Boianai.
Mt. Pudi vicinity seems to be the hovering area.
I myself
stationary white light twice on the same night on April 9th,
in a different place each time.
I believe your students have
sighted one over Boianai.
Bob Smith and Mr. Glover
have all seen it, or similar ones on different occasions - again,
over Boianai, although I think the Baniara people said they watched
it travel across the sky from our direction.
I should think that
this is the first time that the "saucer! has been identified as
I do not doubt the existence of these "things" (indeed
I cannot now that I have seen one for myself) but my simple mind
still requires scientific evidence before I can accept the from-
outer-space-theory.
I am inclined to believe that probably
many UFO's are more likely some form of electric phenomena - or
perhaps something brought about by the atom bomb explosions, etc.
That Stephen should actually make out a saucer could be the work
unconscious mind as it is very likely that at some time he
has seen
illustrations of some kind in a magazine,
or it is very
possible that saucers do exist, but it is only a 50/50 chance
that they are not earth made,
still less that they should carry men
(more likely radio controlled), and it is still unproven that they
are solids.
It is all too difficult to understand for me; I prefer
to wait for some bright boy to catch one to be exhibited in Martin
Square.
Please return this report as I have no copy and I want
Nor, to have it.
Yours,
Doubting William.
REPORT ON UFO SIGHTING
Place: Boianai.
Time: 1 a.m. Sunday, 2lst June, 1959.
Weather Conditions: Patches of low eloud, otherwise clear. Moonlight.
Name of Observer: Stephen Moi, Teacher.
Description: (as told to W.B. Gill by the observer 23/6/59 at Boianad.
On coming out of the house at one o'clock on Sunday
morning (21st June) I saw a bright light (white) silently coming out
of the sky from a point about a quarter of a mile out to sea,
slightly west of the Boianai station.
It descended
from what seemed
a great height and I watched it for
the space of
about three minutes
moving and
descending eastwards and parallel to the coast.
It
stopped at a point a little to the east of the station and at a height
of about three hundred feet.
There it remained stationary for

STATEMENT
Dogura,
9th July 1959.
Last night I went out of my house between 11.30 and
midnight and I saw a bright beam of light shining across
the sea near the shore and parallel to the beach.
The light was white and it lit up waves of the sea.
I thought it was coming from a boat and so I returned to
the house. I heard no noise.
(Dgd.) Kingsford Dibela,
Teacher, Dogura.

Dear Sir,
My Society has been undertaking investigations into
the alleged sightings of unidentified flying objects reported
by the Reverend Father W.B. G111 of Boianai Anglican Mission,
Papua, New Guinea.
Since Father Gill and his many witnesses reported
exchanging signals with humanoid beings aboard the objects,
these sightings, once established as authentic, appear to
furnish the much-coveted proof that the UFO's are intelligently-
guided machines.
Accordingly, I respectfully submit your Department
the following questions, trusting that you will see fit to
assist us in our enquiries:-
1. Has the Department initiated enquiries into Father Gill's
claims?
Il so, has any conclusion been reached as to
the veracity and accuracy of the witnesses?
2. Ii the reports are regarded as genuine, has the Department
arrived at any conclusion as to the nature and origin of
the objects described therein?
3. If the Department has not in fact investigated the reports,
is the Department satisfied that these incidents do not
constitute the infringement of Australian territorial
jurisdiction by air-craft emanating from an alien earth
nation?
Yours truly,
letusshore"
PETER E. NORRIS
Hon. President
LL.B.

3, B
INVESTIGATION OF FLYING SAUCERS
SEARBARNT OF R.A.A.E. POLICY
The R.A.A.P. accepte reports on flying saucera and
attempts an allocation of reliability.
Those that fall in the
reliable class are then subjeoted to further investigation as and
when the opportunity ocours. As a result of this further investigation,
a smaller number of reports are followed up and investigationa are made
with the Meteorological Services, the Government Astronomor and the Civil
Aviation authorities in an attempt to fit the original occurrences in with
ang normal flying activity or meteorological phenomena.
As a result of investigations in the pest, there 1s no
doubt that reliable obeervers have reported sightings which today are
inexplicable within the resources available to
the Reiei.l. Reports
of this type are continuously filed in an attempt to develop sufficient
depth of evidence for acourate analysis to be made.
It may however, be
several years before the required depth of evidence in aveilable.

===== PAGE 229 =====
{"alpha_words": 201, "chars": 1374, "method": "ocr", "page": 229, "variant": "plain-vision"}
1.
2.
REPLY TO QUESTIONNAIRE ON UNIDENTIFLED
FLYING OBJECTS - WORLD VIDE WAGAZINE
Q.
A.
What is the official attitude and policy of
your Government in connection with so-called
flying saucers?
At the time of the spate of flying saucers
reports in 1952/54, the Australian Government
adopted a wait and see' attitude. Reports
on flying saucers from various parts of the
world have been studied and local investigations
have taken place. The Government has adopted
the attitude that there is still insufficient
evidence available to enable an accurate
analysis to be made.
Q.
A.
The United States of America have a special
commission which investigates reliable reports
of unidentified flying objects. Has
your
Government a similar project in operation?
There have been insufficient reports of
unidentified flying objects in Australia to
warrant the formation of a special commission
to investigate them. The Royal Australian
Air Force accerts reports and attempts an
allocation of reliability.
Reports that fall
in the reliable class are subjected to further
we satio
informatien as and when opportunity occurs
As a result of this further investigation a
small number of reports are followed up and
investigations are made with the Meteorological
Serviced, the Government Astronomer and the
Civil Aviation authorities in an attempt to

UNIDENTIFIED FLYING OBJECTS
1. What is the official attitude and policy of your
Government in connection with so-called fl ying
saucers?
2. The United States of America have a special
commission which investigates reliable reports
of unidentified flying objects. Has your Government
a similar project in operation?
3. Have you had reports of UFOs seen over your
country? If so, how many? What proportion of
those investigated have been found to be natural
phenomena, such as balloons, meteors, parhella etc.?
4. Is it possible for you to supply details of any of
the more reliable recent reports of flying saucers
seen over your country?
5= Have you any pictures of alleged flying saucers
which could be made available to me?
6. Any other information you are able to supply on
this subject would be most welcome.

Director,

MELBOURNE.
INVESTIGATIONS IEO REPORTS OF
UNIDENTITIED FLYING OBJECIS
This Department frequently receives reports
direct from civilians, or passed on by other departments,
of unidentified flying objects. We also receive requests
for assistance and advice from various "Flying Saucer
Research Societies".
2.
Many of these reports presumably cover such
mundane things as meteorological and astronomical phenomena;
others appear to be inexplicable. Most of them are outside
the aeronautical field.
3.
As your branch has now established a Scientific
Intelligence Section, it would appear that these reporti
could best be investigated and evaluated by one of your
Scientific Research Officers, who will have a broader back-
ground of knowledge of this type of phenomena than anyone
in this Directorate.
4.
If you agree that you can accept this commit-
ment, I will be glad to make available all the papers which
we have acquired, to date, on this subject.
DIRECTOR
(A.D. HENDERSON)
Group Captain
OF AIR FORCE INTELLIGENCE

6
FLYING SAUCERS
I refer to Wing Cormander Dawsons! DO 1A/1955 of 21 Sep on the
above subject.
I have discussed with the USAT the status of Major Keyhoe.
understand that his book is written in such a way
as to convey the
impression that his statements are based on official documents, and there
is some suggestion that he has made improper use of information to which
he had access while he was serving with the Marine Corps.
he has, how-
ever, no official status whatsoever and a dim view is taken officially of
both him and his works.
Keyhoe and other writers on this subject have caused the USAF a
considerable amount of embarrassment, and I understand that it was largely
for the purpose of refuting their arguments and conclusions that the Air
Force undertook Project Blue Book, a copy of which you will have received
by now. I believe that this publication explains something like 98 percent
cases reported. The Air Force issued it, not only to counter earlier
statements on the subject, but also in the hopes that it would cut the
ground from under a new book by Keyhoe which is soon to be published.
How-
subsequent to its issue the new Secretary of the Air Force refused to
agree to its release to the public. This was the subject of my signal
DJ650 of 17 Oct 55-
As stated in this signal, I have discussed with the USAF the extent
to which the publication can be used by you in replying to the experts who
are worrying you.
They are quite happy to have you use it as a basis for
a statement on the subject, provided you do not directly quote its contents
or refer directly to it or its source.
As the Blue Book has been sent to you, I have not made any request
for individual reports held by the USAF.
However, 1f you still reauire thes,
I shall be pleased to do so.
New reports of unidentified flying objects
are made available to me on the reading panel, and if you wish to have copies
of them, please let me know.
I have not bothered to take them in the past
as I felt that they would be of no use to you.
Wing Commander
RAAF Intelligence Representative
Since writing the above I have received
the attached Sumary which may be of
interest to you.

REPORT ON "FLYING SAUCERS"
INTRODUCTION
In order to assist in assessing two files of reports
held by the Director of Air Force Intelligence concerning
"flying saucers", a general inquiry into the subject matter
was held by the writer.
Among the books investigated were
the following:-
Keyhoe :
"Space Saucers From Outer
Menzel:
"Flying Saucers"
Leslie and Adamski; "glying Saucers Have Landed"
In addition, numerous periodicals and magazines were found to
supply odd reports.
The Vitorian Flying Saucer Investigation Committee
was helpful in supplying further information.
The Committee
not aware of any connection with the R.A.A.F.
3.
Some local sightings of interest were personally
Investigated.
Such investigations were found to render
considerably more information than expected.
Finally,
disoussions were had with various staff members of the
Physios
Department, University of Melbourne.
4.
In this way, it was hoped that a basic understandin
of the problem would
be achieved.
5.
As regards the filed reports, it was the initial
Intention of the writer to analyse the
evidence in an attempt
to understand the basic reason causing the then recent wave
of saucer reports.
However, largely as a result of Keyhoe's
book, it would appear that this evidential analysis 1s no
longer necessary•
Instead, an attempt has been made to
assian each of the various filed reports to some particular
category.
This is a preliminary move in understanding the
method s
and motives of these "saucers" rather than determining
whether they exist or not.
AMERICAN REPORTS
It is worthwhile studying the evidence presented by
This ex-Major of the U.S. Marines purports to
publish U.S. Air Technical Intelligence Reports dealing with
sightings of "Unidentified Flying Objects".
It is almost
inconceivable that this is not an official release, as they
bear the testimony of Albert M. Chop of the
Office of Public
Information, Department of Defence, Washington.
official confirmation
from, say, the U.S. Air Attache or the
Australian Ilaison Officer
in Washington would nevertheless
be highly desirable.
Some of the cases disoussed by Keyhoe are summarised
below (in chronologieal order).
/....2

===== PAGE 262 =====
{"alpha_words": 414, "chars": 2928, "method": "ocr", "page": 262, "variant": "plain-vision"}
2.
1. Norfolk, Virginia - July 14th, 1952, 2112 hours
Pan-American Airways' report to A.T.I.C. of six discs
seen manoeuvring below the aircraft.
Desoription given of an
abrupt change of course, colour changes, high speeds.
Diameter
to thickness ratio about 7/1. Joined by two more dises before
departing.
2. Washington, D.C. - July 20th, 1952, 0040 - 0530 hours
Three radar stations - Air Traffic Control centre at
no am postion, and one or he joke produan e ones
were seen by ground and air observers as coloured lights.
observations continued for five hours during which time the
objects were found
to be capable of linear acceleration in
excess of several 'g'.
They could reverse, perform right-
angled turns and in one instance exceed several thousand m.p.h.
Tney would outdistance any intercepting
'plane, although the
Interceptors could often locate their lights before they went
out of sight. Their departure would be observed on the radar
3. Miohigan - July 29th, 1952, 2140 hours
G.C.I. radar ordered an F.94 to intercept an unknown
objeot flying at 635 m.p.h.
When at 20,000 It.,
the
Interceptor located object
on its radar as being
four miles
listant, same altitude, position marked visually by a light
alternately flashing red, green and white.
The F.94 chased
the objeot at maximum speed for twenty minutes, during which
time the flashing light remained visible in a position corres-
ponding to the alroraft's radar.
The attempted intercept was
tracked by the G.C.I. station.
4. Bellefontaine, Ohio - August Ist, 1952, 1050 hours
G.C.I. radar picked up an unknown moving at 480 m.p.h.
and at great height.
Visually detected by ground observers.
Two F.86's ordered to intercept.
At a ceiling of 40,000 ft.,
one pilot took camera-gun photographs of a bright, round object.
The radar gunsight indicated that the object was 12 - 20,000 ft.
above the observer.
The attempted intercept was traoked by the
G.C.I. radar.

Group

. Oneida Air Foroe Base, Japan - August 5th, 1952, midnight
Round dark object with bright light observed from
control tower with binoculars.
Approached base slowly, hovered
nearby for several minutes, then departed.
Tracked by G.C.I.
radar.
Appeared to split into three units.
7. Gulf of Mex1oo - December 6th, 1952, 0525 hours
3.29 at 18.000 1t.
Four groups each of about five
•blete were observed
at approximately two-minute intervals
by three radar-scopes.
Each group was travelling in excess
of 5,000 m.p.h.
Some of the objects were also sighted
visually.
One group briefly slowed down to airoraft speed,
then aocelerated to its earlier speed and was seen to merge
with a large " ฿11p, the whole then rapidly accelerating to
more than 9,000 m.p.h. before going out of range.
/...3

===== PAGE 263 =====
{"alpha_words": 427, "chars": 2949, "method": "ocr", "page": 263, "variant": "plain-vision"}
3.
CONCLUSIONS
If one assumes these Intelligence Reports are
authentic, then the evidence presented is such that it
difficult
to assume any interpretation other than that
unidentified flying objeots are being observed.
This applies
particularly to those cases where an object is independently
observed to be in a certain position at the one time by means.
of radar, visual and/or photographic methods.
Such combined
evidence
positively eliminates alternative explanations such
as Ispots before the eyes', mass illusions, meteorologioa.
phenomena (including temperature inversion), etc.
In many
the measured velocities and accelerations
are highly
indicative of objects that are intelligently controlled and
whose performance is superior to that known by Western solence.
Indeed, the superiority is such that it is highly improbable
that such objeots have a terrestial origin.
An extra-terrestial origin is preferred to that of a
russian origin as,
even if the Russian forces were so much in
advance of the Western group that they could produce such air-
craft, it is highly improbable that they would send their
secret devices over enemy territory with the attendant risk af
capture or breakdown.
In addition, espionage would surely be
more profitable i1 undertaken by fifth oolumn activities rather
than by the elusive aerial spotting practised by these U.F.0's.
The evidence presented by the reports held by the
R.A.A.F. tend to support the above conclusion - namely, that
certain strange aircraft have been observed to behave in a
manner suggestive of an extra-terrestial origin.
However, as
the Australian evidence is inferior to the American evidence
from the viewpoint of proving the real existence of 'flying
saucers', the writer has contented himself with a modest
attempt at sieving the probable U.F.0, sightings from the
probable 'natural phenomenon! category.
This is done in
Appendix I.
If one could regard this
initial sorting out
being sufficiently reliable,
then reports categorising U.F.O.
should be re-examined in an effort to understand the methods
of propulsion and the motives for the visitations.
that are simply visual have striotly limited analysis value.
Many new reports involving instrumental plotting
are needed.
It is possible to list a number of such recommendations.
RECOMMENDATIONS
1. To increase official interest in U.F.O, reports.
2.
To have at least one full-time investigator who
is encouraged to interview personally any
witnesses involved in an interesting report.
Such interviews tend to uncover considerable
information and can convert a nebulous report
into one suitable for analysis.
3.
Some measure of publicity to encourage people to
submit their reports.
A warning should be given
to potential hoaxers
that aotion will be taken lf
the hoax involves public expense.
4.
Liaison established with the U.S. Air Force
Intelligence with a view towards
exchanging
Information and verifying Keyhoe's claim.
/...4

===== PAGE 264 =====
{"alpha_words": 150, "chars": 975, "method": "ocr", "page": 264, "variant": "plain-vision"}
4.
5. Request Information from the R.A.F. and the
Canadian "Project Magnet".
6. An attempt could be made to form a Scientifie
Advisory Panel to assist in the analysis of
reports, provided that in the future these
reports
are more numerous and of greater detail
then most earlier accounts.
The first three
recommendations should enhance these require-
ments.
The U.S.A.F. and R.A.F. may even be
willing to permit such a panel to examine their
reports.
7.
It is highly recommended that more radar sight-
ings be gained.
One radar report is worth
many unsupported visual reports.
Only radar
can supply range, height, speed and true course.
Visual aids can add shape and colour and, 1f
synchronised with radar, true size.
More radar
stations are needed and
they should operate for
longer periods.
Radar operators should be
briefed as to the procedure to be adopted when
a U.F.O. is suspected.
They should be familiar
with such things as anomalous propagation and
lonised olouds.

APPENDIX II
NOTES ON INDIVIDUAL REPORTS
Report No.1 - Bass Pt., N.S.W. - 16.7.50
1.
If it is assumed that the report is genuine and that it
1s suffiolently acourate to warrant analysis, one is aware of an
inoongruity in the description.
hand, red and green
lights that cirole an area of about a mile in length for about
100 minutes are most unusual according to most U.F.O. reports and
far more indicative of an aeroplane, partloularly as the red
light is to port, and the green to starboard.
On the other hand,
the estimated time for one circuit gives a speed of only 15 m.p.h.
Further, the lights were reported to be stationary at
one period
for five minutes, and
at no time was any noise of an aireraft
heard.
Civil and Air Force authorities were
unaware of the
presence of any airoraft.
If an unauthorised airoraft were to be
Ilying between 0150 and 0555 hours, it is suggestive of subversive
activities.
For this reason alone, further action should have
been taken by E.A.H.Q.
If the report proved to
be a hoax,
litigation could have been invoked to deter future potential
hoaxers.
The subsequent acoount of mysterious car headlights and
a quarry explosion may have been due to entirely unrelated
phenomena.
Prompt investigation would have been able to assist
in that direction.
At this late stage, it would be difficult to
differentiate between
(a) hoax, (b) clandestine operations of a
possibly subversive nature, and (o) unusual behaviour of a U.F.O.

prefer to discard the single meteor idea.
5.
There is a further difficulty in that the colour of the
l1ght is consistently described as a brilllant green-blue.
Although there has been little systematio scientific investigation
predominant green-blue is unusual.
It would be most unusual if
two such meteors were witnessed within ten minutes of each other,
partloularly as a third was seen over Victoria on the next night.
On the other hand, out of the millions of meteors visible
each night, there must oocasionally be some sufficiently unusual
to arouse the interest of the publio.
Unt1l more is known of the
probability of occurrence of unusual meteors, the possibility
remains that two green-blue meteors were seen on 29.11.50 and a
third on 30.11.50.

Report No.9 - Parkes, Sydney : Klama, - 3.5.52, 0610 hours
• An analysis of 15 separate reports indicates that an
object or objects of total apparent size, that of about half full
over N.S.W. at considerable
height and at an elevation
of about 35°
to most observers.
The average duration was about
half a minute.
The descriptions are fairly consistent with a
large slow meteor travelling horizontally or at a shallow angle and
beginning to break up.
Such a meteor should have been visible to
Melbourne residents at an elevation of 10 - 20°.
It is a pity
that Vitorlan newspaper reports failed to supply any details, and
that no official investigations were made in Victoria.
It is somewhat surprising that intense 'flaring' did not
ooour during the break-up, and that the smaller particles did not
fall away more rapidly.
Report No.10 - Woomera - 27.9.52
From the five reports, the observed phenomenon could only
be a meteor or U.F.o.
A meteor that could give rise to the stated
descriptions would have to be rare indeed.
Report No.11 - Woomera - 8.10.52
The velocity and direction of the phenomenon producing
this well-defined radar echo could easily agree with the velocity
and direction of the wind.
The considerable height fluctuations
could conceivably be explained by variable up-currents of hot alr
due to variations in surface contours and in so11 and vegetation
changes.
There was insufficient dataavallable to correlate the
plotted
height variation with a cross-section of the surface
features.
It may be worthwhile doing this.

not be visible to the observer, one could postulate the existence
of a compact highly lonised oloud.
A Layer of snow or 10e
rystals is unlikely as the ground temperature was 84°F an
freezing point would be unlikely at heights of only a few
thousand feet.
It is highly improbable that an ionised cloud
could cross Australia from the Monte Bellds without being dis-
persed, but the recent atomic explosion is suggestive of a
connection.
Perhaps a precipitation of radio-active rain which
vapourised
shortly before reaching the ground could produce a
Locally-lonised cloud.
The observed apparent breaking away of
segments of the radar
echo would indicate that 11
due to a oloud then it would be relatively short-lived.

organizations to us is carefully treated on an equal basis.

1A
247
DRAFE
INVESTIGATION OF FLYING SAUCERS
SLATE OF R.A.A.L. FORCE
1.
The R.A.A.F, accepta reports on flying saucers and
attempts an allocation of reliability. Those that fall in the
reliably class are then subjectato further investigation as
and when the opportunity occurs. As a result of this further
invostigation, a smaller mumber of reports are followed up and
investigations are made with the Meteorological Services, the
Government Astronomer and the Civil Aviation authorities in
an attempt to fit the original occurrences in with any normal
flying activity or meteorological phenomena.
2.
As a result of investigations in the past, there is
no doubt that reliable observers have reported sightings which
today are inexplicable within the resources available to the
R,A,A,T. Reports of this type are continuously filed in an
attempt to develop sufficient depth of evidence for accurate
analysis to be made. It may however, be several years before
*. the required depth of evidence is available.
JAFT
bett
74/14

28th January 1954
Dear Sir,
From time to time people report having seen
unexplained objects in the sky which have cone to be called
"flying saucers",
There have been many explenations of
them - the sun shining on high flying aircraft - meteorologica]
balLoons - imagination, etc.
There in another possib111ty
that may account for some of them, which has not beer
suggested, so far as I know.
On mown dates in each years
the earth
passes through meteor atreams.
These are the
remains of meteore that have daintegrated in outer space,
but which continue their original paths round the sun in
the form of streams of solid particles and meteorie dust.
The faot that the earth moves round the sun in a fixed path
and that these dehris of meteors also move round the sun in
means that the paths of the earth and of some
01 else meteoro strettersect each other on a seroe
of dates which are the same each year.
The earth sometimes
takes a mumber of days to pass through the more important
of these meteoric streame.
The dates of maxima meteorie
netivity are - 3rd January, 21st Apry 10th October,
4th way, oth June,
28th July,
30th beane 1-10th lovember, 14th Bovember, 16th lovenber»
20-23rđ
October,
11-13th
December.
I have liste of the dates over the last several
yenrs on which people have reported having seen "flying
saucera" in lustralia, and have compared them with the dates
on which the earth peases through the principel meteorie
streams.
There appesre to be a noticeable relationahip
between
these two seta of dates. By this I mean that there
tends to be a grouping of flying saucer sightings round
about the meteor-stream dates that it is hard to believe 1a
by chance.
And again, there is a relative absence of
"flying saucer" reports during the principal gaps between
meteor-stream dates.
For Instance, the minimm meteorie
activity of
the year ia between January and April.
are also the months in which fewest "flying saucers" have
been reported in Augtralia.
I do not suggest that this
sore then & pertial explanation of flying saucer phenomena,
nore than a pertial explanation or
A11 that I suggest is thet there is some evidence that
people seem to report otherwise unexplained objects in the
sky round about the dates on which the earth passes througa
ine principal meteor-streams, and there seems to be a 1u1i
in the reporting of flying saucers in between these dates.
I do not suggest any more than that what I have
said may be an explenation of at leget a proportion of the
"flying saucer" reporte.
I alle
Yours very ray,
RG. CASEY
(B. G. CASEY)
The Editor,
"The Sydney Morning Herald",

Mr. Downer, M.P. is going to ask the Minister the following
question in the House at 10 am tomorrow morning.
Last year I asked the Honourable Gentleman a question
about flying saucers to which he gave a facetious reply.
Is the Minister aware of the attention now being devoted in Canada
and by the R.A.F. in England to the nature and origin of these
mysterious objeats? Are similar investigations contemplated by
the R.A.A.I. or does the Minister still regard flying saucers as a
problem for Paychologists rather than the Defence authorities?

Attn: Wing Commender R.A.G. Ellen, M.B.E.
Dear Wing Commander Ellen:
Thank you very much for your letter of 6 July in which you
inclosed copies of previous correspondence in connection with oitings
of unidentified flying objects.
As I mentioned to you when
I was in your office on 18 May, my
headquarters is very interested in receiving reports of all unusual
sightings not only in this area but all over the world. My headquarters
has requested that, if possible, the following infornation should be
reported on any unusual sighting:
1. Electrical Means. (This should be reported immediately upon
sighting.) Reports forwarded ty this method will include,
Insofar as possible:
A brief description of the objects); shape, size, color,
number, formation if more than one, aerodynamie features,
trail of exhaust, propulsion systen, speed, sound,
maneuvers, manner of disappearances, and other pertinent
or unusual features.
/ b. Time of sighting in 24-hour clook zonal time, and length
of time observed.
/
0.
Manner of observations visual or eleotronio, from air
(give speed, altitude, and type of aireraft), or surface.
Any type of optical or electronic equipment used should be
described.
/
đ.
Location of observer during sighting, giving exact
latitude and longitude as closely as feasible, and/or
reference to a known landmark.
Locatior of objects)
with
respeot to observer, giving distance, direction, and
altitude.
e.
Identifying infornation of observer (s) and witness (es),
estimate of reliability and experience, and any factors
bearing on estimated reliability of the sighting.

sightings.

INSTRUCTIONS TO BE OBSERVED IN THE COMPILATION AND
USE OF FILES
I. When opening a new file, care must be taken to see that a file
dealing with the subject is not already in existence and covers
should be completed with details for which space is provided.
2. Papers are to be attached to the file cover by means of paper fasten-
ers of appropriate size, and washers, when available, are also to be
used.
Insert paper fasteners through the front and back covers
of the file at the point indicated thereon.
A t-in. fastener is to
be used for minute sheets and a fastener of not more than one-
and-a-half inches to attach enclosures.
When there are as many
enclosures on a file as can conveniently be fastened by a lf-in.
paper fastener, a new part should be opened.
3. Minute sheets are to be attached to the left-hand inside cover and
enclosures to the right-hand side.
4. Minute sheets are to be used for correspondence between branches
and sections.
Correspondence from and to outside bodies is to
appear as an enclosure.
If the enclosure is in one part, its number
will be followed by the letter "A", subsequent parts being lettered
"B":
"C"*
"D"
' &c.
5. Minute Number I is to be on the printed side of the minute sheet
and is to face the cover on the minute side of the file.
6. Wherever possible minutes should be made on the file. If a loose
minute is used the file number should be quoted and the minute
attached to the file when available.
. Minutes must be added in numerical order and both sides of the
minute sheets are to be usec
8. Files must not be passed between Branches by hand without transit
slips.
9.. Notes of action taken may be entered on the minute sheets, other-
wise unaddressed minutes will not be made.
10. Mierese to them at he minice head and such words as agree
such words as "agree"
and "concur" may be used to obviate separate minutes by individual
addressees. An officer's initials so placed will indicate concurrence
with the minute,
I on the cover is to be indicated the persons for whom
enclosures are intended, the number of that minute or
nd the date it is passed out from the last person noting

person indicated in Column I has taken the necessary

to pass: completing Columns 2 and 3.

covers or enclosures are damaged the necessary repairs
made before the file is passed on.
